<a id='question-145'></a>

### Question 145: Hypersensitivity and Autoimmune Disease: Part 3 Focus 45 (powerful)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathogenesis of Type III hypersensitivity reactions, specifically immune complex deposition leading to vasculitis, and differentiating it from other hypersensitivity types and autoimmune diseases. (理解III型过敏反应的发病机制，特别是免疫复合物沉积导致血管炎，并将其与其他过敏反应类型和自身免疫性疾病区分开来。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and chronic kidney disease presents to the emergency department with a 3-day history of fever, malaise, arthralgias, and a palpable purpuric rash on his lower extremities. He recently started a new medication, ampicillin, for a presumed urinary tract infection. Physical examination reveals scattered petechiae and purpura, particularly on the lower legs, along with mild joint swelling in the wrists and ankles. Laboratory studies show elevated erythrocyte sedimentation rate (ESR), C-reactive protein (CRP), and decreased serum complement levels (C3 and C4). A skin biopsy from a purpuric lesion shows leukocytoclastic vasculitis with fibrinoid necrosis and deposition of immunoglobulin G (IgG) and C3 in the vessel walls. Urinalysis reveals proteinuria and hematuria. Renal biopsy shows crescent formation in several glomeruli. Which of the following is the most likely underlying immunological mechanism responsible for this patient's clinical presentation?

(A) Antibody-mediated cytotoxicity against glomerular basement membrane antigens.
(B) Deposition of immune complexes containing anti-nuclear antibodies (ANA) in small vessel walls.
(C) T-cell mediated cytotoxicity leading to direct damage of glomerular cells.
(D) Activation of mast cells and basophils by IgE antibodies bound to drug haptens.
(E) Formation of anti-glomerular basement membrane (anti-GBM) antibodies leading to linear IgG deposition.

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male with hypertension and chronic kidney disease (CKD). These comorbidities can predispose to certain conditions or complicate presentations.
*   **Presenting Symptoms:** Fever, malaise, arthralgias, palpable purpuric rash (lower extremities). This constellation suggests a systemic inflammatory process, potentially vasculitis.
*   **Recent Exposure:** Started ampicillin recently. This is a crucial clue, suggesting a possible drug-induced reaction.
*   **Physical Exam:** Petechiae, purpura (lower legs), joint swelling (wrists, ankles). Confirms vasculitis and arthritis.
*   **Laboratory Findings:** Elevated ESR/CRP (inflammation), decreased C3/C4 (complement consumption). Complement consumption is characteristic of immune complex-mediated processes.
*   **Skin Biopsy:** Leukocytoclastic vasculitis, fibrinoid necrosis, IgG and C3 deposition in vessel walls. This is pathognomonic for immune complex deposition in small vessels.
*   **Urinalysis:** Proteinuria, hematuria. Indicates renal involvement (glomerulonephritis).
*   **Renal Biopsy:** Crescent formation. Suggests rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis (RPGN), often associated with immune complex deposition or anti-GBM disease.
*   **Overall Picture:** The patient presents with systemic symptoms (fever, malaise, arthralgias), skin manifestations (palpable purpura), renal involvement (proteinuria, hematuria, crescentic glomerulonephritis), and laboratory evidence of inflammation and complement consumption, triggered by a new medication (ampicillin). This clinical picture is highly suggestive of a drug-induced systemic vasculitis, likely mediated by immune complexes.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **Type III Hypersensitivity:** This reaction involves the formation of antigen-antibody (immune) complexes. These complexes deposit in tissues (e.g., blood vessel walls, glomeruli, joints), activate complement (leading to C3a, C5a production), and recruit inflammatory cells (neutrophils). Neutrophils release lysosomal enzymes and reactive oxygen species, causing tissue damage (vasculitis, glomerulonephritis).
*   **Drug-Induced Immune Complex Vasculitis:** Certain drugs (like penicillin, sulfonamides, cephalosporins, NSAIDs) can act as haptens, binding to serum proteins or self-antigens, forming neoantigens. Antibodies (often IgG) are then produced against these neoantigens, forming immune complexes.
*   **Pathology:** The deposition of these immune complexes in small blood vessels leads to leukocytoclastic vasculitis (inflammation and destruction of vessel walls by neutrophils), characterized histologically by fibrinoid necrosis, neutrophil infiltration, and extravasation of red blood cells (causing purpura).
*   **Complement Consumption:** Activation of the classical complement pathway by immune complexes leads to consumption of C3 and C4, explaining the decreased serum levels.
*   **Clinical Manifestations:** Systemic symptoms (fever, malaise), skin lesions (palpable purpura), arthralgias/arthritis, and organ involvement (kidneys - glomerulonephritis, lungs - pulmonary vasculitis, nerves - peripheral neuropathy) are common.
*   **Mechanism in this Case:** Ampicillin likely acted as a hapten, forming immune complexes with antibodies. These complexes deposited in small vessels (skin, kidneys, joints), activating complement and causing inflammation and tissue damage. The decreased C3/C4 and IgG/C3 deposition in the skin biopsy strongly support this mechanism.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Antibody-mediated cytotoxicity against glomerular basement membrane antigens:** This describes Goodpasture syndrome or anti-GBM disease. While it causes glomerulonephritis and sometimes pulmonary hemorrhage, it is characterized by *linear* IgG deposition along the GBM on immunofluorescence, not granular deposition typical of immune complexes. Also, it doesn't typically cause palpable purpura or arthralgias as prominent features.
- (C) **T-cell mediated cytotoxicity leading to direct damage of glomerular cells:** This mechanism is involved in certain types of glomerulonephritis (e.g., T-cell mediated GN), but it doesn't explain the palpable purpura, systemic symptoms, decreased complement levels, or the characteristic granular IgG/C3 deposition seen in the biopsy. T-cell mediated reactions are typically Type IV hypersensitivity.
- (D) **Activation of mast cells and basophils by IgE antibodies bound to drug haptens:** This describes a Type I hypersensitivity reaction (immediate hypersensitivity, allergy). It typically presents with urticaria, angioedema, bronchospasm, or anaphylaxis, not palpable purpura, vasculitis, glomerulonephritis, or decreased complement levels. IgE is the primary antibody involved, not IgG.
- (E) **Formation of anti-glomerular basement membrane (anti-GBM) antibodies leading to linear IgG deposition:** This is the mechanism for anti-GBM disease (Goodpasture syndrome), as described in (A). The key difference is the *pattern* of IgG deposition (linear vs. granular) and the typical clinical presentation (pulmonary hemorrhage is common, palpable purpura is not).

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Drug-Induced Immune Complex Vasculitis (Correct Answer) | Anti-GBM Disease (Goodpasture Syndrome) | Type I Hypersensitivity (Drug Allergy) | T-Cell Mediated GN |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------- | :----------------- |
| **Hypersensitivity Type** | III                                                    | II (Antibody-mediated cytotoxicity)      | I                                      | IV                 |
| **Antibody Type**       | IgG (forming complexes)                                | IgG (anti-GBM)                           | IgE                                     | N/A                |
| **Antigen**             | Drug-protein complex / Neoantigen                       | Glomerular Basement Membrane             | Drug hapten bound to carrier protein   | Self-antigen       |
| **Immune Complex Deposition** | Granular (skin, kidney, joints)                       | Linear (GBM)                             | N/A                                     | N/A                |
| **Complement Levels**   | Decreased (C3, C4)                                     | Normal or slightly decreased             | Normal                                  | Normal             |
| **Key Pathology**       | Leukocytoclastic vasculitis, fibrinoid necrosis         | Linear IgG deposition, crescent formation | Mast cell degranulation, edema         | T-cell infiltrate  |
| **Typical Presentation** | Purpura, arthralgia, fever, glomerulonephritis         | RPGN, pulmonary hemorrhage              | Urticaria, anaphylaxis                 | Variable           |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Drug-induced immune complex vasculitis is a classic example of Type III hypersensitivity. Key features include palpable purpura, arthralgias, glomerulonephritis, decreased complement levels, and granular immune complex deposition on biopsy. Differentiate it from Type I (IgE-mediated allergy), Type II (anti-GBM disease - linear deposition), and Type IV (T-cell mediated) hypersensitivity reactions. Always consider drug exposure in patients presenting with vasculitis.

### Question 146: Hypersensitivity and Autoimmune Disease: Part 3 Focus 46 (chemicals)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Drug-induced hypersensitivity reactions, specifically Type II hypersensitivity mediated by drug-dependent antibodies leading to hemolytic anemia. (药物诱导的过敏反应，特别是通过药物依赖性抗体介导的II型过敏反应导致溶血性贫血。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of rheumatoid arthritis is being treated with intravenous ceftriaxone for a community-acquired pneumonia. Three days into the ceftriaxone infusion, he develops fever, chills, and dark urine. Laboratory tests reveal a hemoglobin level of 8.5 g/dL (normal 13.5-17.5 g/dL), elevated indirect bilirubin, and decreased haptoglobin. A direct antiglobulin test (DAT), also known as the Coombs test, is positive. Peripheral blood smear shows spherocytes. The patient's rheumatoid arthritis is well-controlled on methotrexate and hydroxychloroquine. He has no known allergies to other medications. Which of the following mechanisms is most likely responsible for this patient's hemolytic anemia?

(A) Formation of immune complexes that activate complement, leading to phagocytosis of erythrocytes by macrophages in the spleen.
(B) Direct binding of drug-specific IgG antibodies to erythrocyte surface antigens, leading to complement activation and phagocytosis.
(C) Formation of drug-dependent antibodies that bind to erythrocytes only in the presence of the drug, leading to complement activation and extravascular hemolysis.
(D) Formation of drug-dependent IgM antibodies that bind to erythrocytes only in the presence of the drug, leading to agglutination and intravascular hemolysis.
(E) Formation of drug-dependent antibodies that bind to erythrocytes only in the presence of the drug, leading to antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) by NK cells.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male with rheumatoid arthritis (autoimmune disease, potentially immunocompromised or on immunosuppressants like methotrexate/hydroxychloroquine).
- **Trigger:** Intravenous ceftriaxone infusion (a cephalosporin antibiotic).
- **Symptoms:** Fever, chills, dark urine (suggestive of hemolysis).
- **Lab Findings:**
    - Low hemoglobin (8.5 g/dL): Anemia.
    - Elevated indirect bilirubin: Increased breakdown of heme from red blood cells.
    - Decreased haptoglobin: Haptoglobin binds free hemoglobin; decreased levels indicate intravascular hemolysis.
    - Positive Direct Antiglobulin Test (DAT/Coombs test): Indicates antibodies or complement are bound to the patient's red blood cells.
    - Peripheral blood smear: Spherocytes (suggestive of extravascular hemolysis, often seen in autoimmune hemolytic anemia).
- **Diagnosis:** The constellation of symptoms, lab findings (anemia, elevated indirect bilirubin, low haptoglobin, positive DAT), and spherocytes strongly points towards drug-induced immune hemolytic anemia (DIHA).
- **Question:** Asks for the specific mechanism of this DIHA.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Drug-Induced Immune Hemolytic Anemia (DIHA):** This is a form of Type II hypersensitivity reaction. It occurs when a drug (like ceftriaxone) adsorbs onto the surface of red blood cells (RBCs). This drug-modified RBC is then recognized as foreign by the immune system.
- **Antibody Formation:** The patient develops antibodies (typically IgG, but sometimes IgM) against the drug *only when it is bound to the RBC surface*. These antibodies are called drug-dependent antibodies.
- **Mechanism:**
    - The drug-dependent antibodies bind to the drug-coated RBCs.
    - This binding can activate the complement system (classical pathway) via the Fc region of the bound IgG.
    - Complement activation leads to the formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC), causing intravascular hemolysis (though extravascular hemolysis is also common).
    - Opsonization of RBCs by complement components (e.g., C3b) or by the Fc portion of the IgG antibody leads to phagocytosis of the coated RBCs by macrophages, primarily in the spleen (extravascular hemolysis).
    - The positive DAT confirms the presence of antibodies or complement on the RBC surface.
- **Distinguishing DIHA from Autoimmune Hemolytic Anemia (AIHA):** In AIHA, antibodies target intrinsic RBC antigens, and the DAT is positive even without drug exposure. In DIHA, the antibodies target the drug *on* the RBC surface, and the DAT is typically positive only when the drug is present (though sometimes it can be positive even after the drug is removed).
- **Clinical Presentation:** Symptoms often appear days to weeks after starting the drug. The positive DAT is crucial for diagnosis. Spherocytes are often seen due to partial phagocytosis of the RBC membrane by splenic macrophages (extravascular hemolysis).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Formation of immune complexes that activate complement, leading to phagocytosis of erythrocytes by macrophages in the spleen. This describes a Type III hypersensitivity reaction mechanism (immune complex deposition) or potentially a component of some autoimmune hemolytic anemias, but it's not the primary mechanism for *drug-induced* hemolytic anemia where the drug acts as a hapten on the RBC surface. The drug itself isn't forming circulating immune complexes with RBC antigens.
- (B): Direct binding of drug-specific IgG antibodies to erythrocyte surface antigens, leading to complement activation and phagocytosis. This describes a mechanism where the antibody targets an *intrinsic* RBC antigen, which is characteristic of autoimmune hemolytic anemia (AIHA), not drug-induced hemolytic anemia (DIHA). In DIHA, the antibody targets the drug *adsorbed* onto the RBC surface, not an inherent RBC antigen.
- (C): Formation of drug-dependent antibodies that bind to erythrocytes only in the presence of the drug, leading to complement activation and extravascular hemolysis. This accurately describes the mechanism of DIHA. The antibodies are specific for the drug-RBC complex. Binding leads to complement activation (often C3b deposition) and opsonization, resulting in phagocytosis by splenic macrophages (extravascular hemolysis). While intravascular hemolysis can also occur, extravascular is a major component, and spherocytes on the smear support this. The key is the *drug-dependent* nature of the antibody binding.
- (D): Formation of drug-dependent IgM antibodies that bind to erythrocytes only in the presence of the drug, leading to agglutination and intravascular hemolysis. While IgM antibodies can be involved in DIHA, they are less common than IgG. IgM typically causes agglutination and complement activation, leading primarily to *intravascular* hemolysis. The clinical picture (spherocytes suggesting extravascular hemolysis) and the typical antibody isotype (IgG) make this less likely than option C. Also, the question asks for the *most likely* mechanism.
- (E): Formation of drug-dependent antibodies that bind to erythrocytes only in the presence of the drug, leading to antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) by NK cells. ADCC is mediated by IgG binding to target cells (in this case, drug-coated RBCs) and then binding to Fc receptors on NK cells, leading to NK cell activation and lysis. While ADCC can occur in DIHA, complement activation and phagocytosis (as described in C) are considered the more common and primary mechanisms, especially given the positive DAT and spherocytes.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Drug-Induced Hemolytic Anemia (DIHA) | Autoimmune Hemolytic Anemia (AIHA) | Immune Complex Disease (e.g., SLE) |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :--------------------------------- | :--------------------------------- |
| **Target Antigen**       | Drug adsorbed on RBC surface         | Intrinsic RBC antigen              | Circulating antigens (e.g., DNA)   |
| **Antibody Type**       | Drug-dependent (usually IgG)         | Autoantibody (IgG or IgM)          | IgG or IgM                        |
| **DAT Result**          | Positive (drug present)              | Positive (drug absent)             | Usually Negative                   |
| **Mechanism**           | Complement activation, Phagocytosis  | Complement activation, Phagocytosis | Immune complex deposition          |
| **Clinical Context**    | Recent drug exposure                 | Often chronic, underlying disease  | Systemic autoimmune disease        |
| **Key Feature**         | Drug dependence of antibody binding  | Autoantibody targets RBC itself   | Circulating immune complexes       |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Drug-induced hemolytic anemia is a Type II hypersensitivity reaction mediated by drug-dependent antibodies (usually IgG) that bind to RBCs only when the drug is present. This leads to complement activation and/or phagocytosis, resulting in hemolysis. Differentiating DIHA from AIHA relies on the drug dependence of the antibody binding and the timing relative to drug exposure. Key differentials include AIHA and other causes of hemolytic anemia.

<a id='question-147'></a>

### Question 147: Hypersensitivity and Autoimmune Disease: Part 3 Focus 47 (Figure)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathogenesis of Type III hypersensitivity reactions, specifically immune complex deposition leading to tissue damage, and differentiating it from other hypersensitivity types and autoimmune diseases. (理解III型过敏反应的发病机制，特别是免疫复合物沉积导致组织损伤，并将其与其他过敏反应类型和自身免疫性疾病区分开来。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and chronic kidney disease (CKD) stage 3 presents to the emergency department with fever, malaise, and a purpuric rash on his lower extremities. He was recently started on a new medication, minocycline, for acne rosacea. Laboratory tests reveal elevated serum creatinine (2.5 mg/dL, baseline 1.5 mg/dL), proteinuria (3+ on dipstick), and decreased complement C3 levels (60 mg/dL, normal range 85-160 mg/dL). Urinalysis shows red blood cell casts. A skin biopsy from the purpuric rash reveals fibrinoid necrosis and deposition of IgG and C3 in small vessel walls. Renal biopsy shows similar findings with immune complex deposition in the glomeruli. The patient's anti-nuclear antibody (ANA) test is negative, and rheumatoid factor (RF) is negative.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying immunological mechanism responsible for this patient's clinical presentation?

(A) Antibody-mediated cytotoxicity against glomerular basement membrane antigens.
(B) Deposition of pre-formed immune complexes leading to complement activation and neutrophil recruitment.
(C) T-cell mediated cytotoxicity against renal tubular epithelial cells.
(D) IgE-mediated mast cell degranulation causing systemic vasodilation and increased vascular permeability.
(E) Direct binding of autoantibodies to cell surface receptors, leading to cellular dysfunction.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male with CKD and hypertension. This suggests pre-existing renal vulnerability.
*   **Trigger:** New medication (minocycline). This points towards a drug-induced reaction.
*   **Symptoms:** Fever, malaise, purpuric rash. These are systemic symptoms suggestive of inflammation and vasculitis.
*   **Lab Findings:** Elevated creatinine, proteinuria, decreased C3, RBC casts. These indicate acute kidney injury (AKI) superimposed on CKD, likely glomerulonephritis, with complement consumption.
*   **Biopsy Findings (Skin & Kidney):** Fibrinoid necrosis, IgG and C3 deposition in small vessel walls/glomeruli. This is classic evidence of immune complex deposition and vasculitis.
*   **Negative Autoantibody Tests:** ANA and RF negative rule out common systemic autoimmune diseases like SLE or rheumatoid arthritis as the primary cause, although drug-induced lupus is a possibility, the pattern here is more consistent with serum sickness-like reaction.
*   **Overall Picture:** The constellation of symptoms (fever, rash, arthritis - although not explicitly mentioned, often present), AKI with glomerulonephritis, low C3, and evidence of immune complex deposition in tissues following exposure to a new drug (minocycline) is highly characteristic of a Type III hypersensitivity reaction, specifically serum sickness or drug-induced serum sickness-like reaction.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **Type III Hypersensitivity:** Mediated by immune complexes (antigen-antibody complexes, typically IgG or IgM).
*   **Pathogenesis:**
    1.  **Antigen Exposure:** The patient is exposed to minocycline.
    2.  **Antibody Formation:** The patient develops antibodies (IgG) against minocycline or a minocycline-protein adduct.
    3.  **Immune Complex Formation:** These antibodies bind to the drug antigen, forming circulating immune complexes.
    4.  **Immune Complex Deposition:** These complexes deposit in various tissues, particularly small blood vessels (skin, kidneys, joints), where the antigen concentration is high or where the vessel walls are permeable.
    5.  **Complement Activation:** Deposited immune complexes activate the classical complement pathway (C1q, C4, C2, C3 convertase, C5 convertase).
    6.  **Inflammation:** Complement activation generates anaphylatoxins (C3a, C5a) that recruit neutrophils and other inflammatory cells. C5b-9 (MAC) formation can also contribute to tissue damage.
    7.  **Tissue Damage:** Neutrophils release proteases and reactive oxygen species, causing inflammation, fibrinoid necrosis, and tissue injury (vasculitis, glomerulonephritis).
    8.  **Complement Consumption:** Extensive complement activation leads to decreased serum levels of complement components, particularly C3 and C4, as seen in this patient.
*   **Serum Sickness:** A classic example of Type III hypersensitivity, often occurring 1-2 weeks after exposure to foreign proteins (e.g., antivenom, certain drugs). Drug-induced serum sickness-like reactions can occur with various medications, including minocycline.
*   **Key Features:** Systemic symptoms (fever, rash, arthralgia), glomerulonephritis, vasculitis, low complement levels, evidence of immune complex deposition on biopsy.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Antibody-mediated cytotoxicity against glomerular basement membrane antigens:** This describes Type II hypersensitivity, specifically Goodpasture syndrome or anti-GBM disease. While it causes glomerulonephritis, it involves antibodies directly targeting the basement membrane, not immune complex deposition. Complement activation occurs, but the primary mechanism is different, and the biopsy would show linear IgG deposition along the GBM, not granular deposition characteristic of immune complexes.
*   **(C) T-cell mediated cytotoxicity against renal tubular epithelial cells:** This describes a Type IV hypersensitivity reaction (cell-mediated immunity) or a T-cell mediated autoimmune process like certain forms of tubulointerstitial nephritis. It does not typically involve immune complex deposition, vasculitis, or significant complement consumption. Biopsy would show T-cell infiltrates in the interstitium.
*   **(D) IgE-mediated mast cell degranulation causing systemic vasodilation and increased vascular permeability:** This describes Type I hypersensitivity (immediate hypersensitivity, allergy, anaphylaxis). It involves IgE antibodies, mast cells, and histamine release. While it can cause systemic symptoms, it does not typically cause immune complex deposition, vasculitis, glomerulonephritis, or low complement levels.
*   **(E) Direct binding of autoantibodies to cell surface receptors, leading to cellular dysfunction:** This describes Type II hypersensitivity, specifically receptor-mediated disease (e.g., Graves' disease, myasthenia gravis). Antibodies bind to receptors, either stimulating or blocking them. It does not involve immune complex deposition or the typical features of serum sickness.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Correct Answer (B) - Type III Hypersensitivity (Immune Complex) | Option (A) - Type II (Anti-GBM) | Option (C) - Type IV (Cell-Mediated) | Option (D) - Type I (IgE) | Option (E) - Type II (Receptor) |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------- | :---------------------------------- | :---------------------- | :----------------------------- |
| **Mediator**            | Immune Complexes (IgG/IgM)                                      | IgG/IgM Antibodies             | T-cells                             | IgE Antibodies          | IgG Antibodies                 |
| **Target**              | Tissue Vessels/Glomeruli (deposition site)                      | Cell Surface Antigens (GBM)    | Intracellular/Cell Surface Antigens | Mast Cells/Basophils    | Cell Surface Receptors         |
| **Mechanism**           | Complement Activation, Neutrophil Recruitment                  | Complement/ADCC, Cell Lysis    | Cytokine Release, Cell Lysis        | Mast Cell Degranulation | Receptor Blockade/Stimulation  |
| **Key Findings**        | Immune Complex Deposition, Vasculitis, Glomerulonephritis, Low C3 | Linear IgG on GBM, Crescentic GN | T-cell Infiltrates, Granulomas      | Wheal & Flare, Anaphylaxis | Specific Receptor Ab          |
| **Clinical Example**    | Serum Sickness, Drug Reaction (Minocycline)                     | Goodpasture Syndrome           | Contact Dermatitis, T1DM            | Asthma, Anaphylaxis     | Graves' Disease, Myasthenia Gravis |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Type III hypersensitivity reactions are characterized by the deposition of circulating immune complexes in tissues, leading to complement activation, inflammation, and tissue damage (e.g., vasculitis, glomerulonephritis). Key features include systemic symptoms, evidence of immune complex deposition on biopsy, and often decreased complement levels. Differentiating Type III from Type II (antibody against cell surface/matrix) and Type IV (cell-mediated) reactions is crucial based on the underlying mechanism and histological findings. Remember serum sickness and drug reactions are classic examples.

<a id='question-148'></a>

### Question 148: Hypersensitivity and Autoimmune Disease: Part 3 Focus 48 (Development)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological basis of graft-versus-host disease (GVHD), specifically the roles of T cells (CD4+ and CD8+) and antigen-presenting cells (APCs) in recognizing host antigens as foreign, leading to tissue damage. (理解移植物抗宿主病 (GVHD) 的免疫学基础，特别是 T 细胞 (CD4+ 和 CD8+) 和抗原提呈细胞 (APCs) 在识别宿主抗原为外来物并导致组织损伤中的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 55-year-old male with relapsed acute myeloid leukemia (AML) undergoes allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (HSCT) from an HLA-matched unrelated donor. The conditioning regimen includes fludarabine and total body irradiation. Post-transplant, he develops pancytopenia, which is initially attributed to graft failure. However, 3 weeks later, he develops a diffuse maculopapular rash, severe watery diarrhea, and painful oral ulcerations. Liver function tests show elevated bilirubin and alkaline phosphatase. Skin biopsy reveals apoptotic keratinocytes and lymphocytic infiltrate at the dermoepidermal junction. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood shows a significant increase in CD3+ cells, with a predominance of CD4+ T cells expressing HLA-DR and CD25, and CD8+ T cells expressing HLA-DR and CD107a. Serum levels of IL-2 and IFN-γ are markedly elevated.

Which of the following cellular interactions is MOST critical for the pathogenesis of this patient's condition?

(A) CD4+ T cells recognizing host MHC class II antigens presented by donor APCs.
(B) CD8+ T cells recognizing host MHC class I antigens presented by donor APCs.
(C) CD4+ T cells recognizing host MHC class II antigens presented by host APCs.
(D) CD8+ T cells recognizing host MHC class I antigens presented by host APCs.
(E) CD4+ T cells recognizing donor MHC class II antigens presented by host APCs.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 55-year-old male with relapsed AML undergoing allogeneic HSCT. This sets the stage for potential GVHD.
- **Conditioning Regimen:** Fludarabine and total body irradiation. This regimen is immunosuppressive but doesn't prevent GVHD, especially with mismatched minor histocompatibility antigens or when the host has residual leukemic cells.
- **Post-Transplant Course:** Initial pancytopenia (could be graft failure or GVHD), followed by rash, diarrhea, oral ulcerations, and liver dysfunction (elevated bilirubin, ALP) 3 weeks later. This constellation of symptoms is classic for acute GVHD affecting the skin, gut, and liver.
- **Skin Biopsy:** Apoptotic keratinocytes and lymphocytic infiltrate at the dermoepidermal junction are characteristic histological findings of cutaneous GVHD.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Increased CD3+ cells (T cells), predominance of CD4+ T cells (expressing activation markers HLA-DR and CD25) and CD8+ T cells (expressing activation markers HLA-DR and CD107a). This indicates activation and proliferation of both helper and cytotoxic T cells from the donor graft.
- **Cytokine Levels:** Elevated IL-2 (T cell growth factor) and IFN-γ (Th1 cytokine, promotes cell-mediated immunity) further support T cell activation and involvement in the pathogenesis.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST critical cellular interaction driving the pathogenesis. This requires understanding the mechanism of GVHD.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Graft-versus-Host Disease (GVHD):** Occurs when immunocompetent T cells from the donor graft recognize the recipient's (host's) tissues as foreign and mount an immune attack.
- **Mechanism:** Donor T cells (CD4+ and CD8+) present in the graft recognize host antigens (MHC molecules and minor histocompatibility antigens) presented by host antigen-presenting cells (APCs) like dendritic cells, macrophages, and B cells.
- **Key Players:**
    - **Donor T cells:** Both CD4+ (helper) and CD8+ (cytotoxic) T cells are involved. CD4+ T cells recognize host MHC class II molecules presenting peptides, while CD8+ T cells recognize host MHC class I molecules presenting peptides. They also recognize minor histocompatibility antigens presented by either MHC class I or II.
    - **Host APCs:** These cells process and present host antigens (including MHC molecules and peptides derived from host proteins) to the donor T cells.
    - **Antigens:**
        - **Major Histocompatibility Antigens (MHC):** Differences in MHC molecules between donor and host are the primary targets, especially in allogeneic transplants.
        - **Minor Histocompatibility Antigens:** Peptides derived from polymorphic host proteins that are presented by MHC molecules. These are often the main targets in HLA-matched transplants.
- **Pathogenesis Steps:**
    1. Donor T cells (CD4+ and CD8+) are infused into the recipient.
    2. Host APCs process and present host antigens (MHC molecules and peptides) to the donor T cells.
    3. Donor T cells recognize these host antigens as foreign via their T cell receptors (TCRs).
    4. Activated donor T cells proliferate and differentiate into effector cells (cytotoxic T lymphocytes - CTLs, and helper T cells - Th cells).
    5. Effector T cells migrate to target tissues (skin, gut, liver) and cause inflammation and damage. CD8+ CTLs directly kill host cells presenting foreign antigens. CD4+ Th cells release cytokines (like IFN-γ, TNF-α) that activate macrophages and contribute to inflammation and tissue damage.
- **Focus on the MOST critical interaction:** The core event initiating GVHD is the recognition of host antigens by donor T cells. This recognition occurs when host APCs present host antigens (including MHC molecules and peptides) to the donor T cells. Therefore, the interaction between donor T cells and host APCs presenting host antigens is crucial.

##### 3. Analysis of Options
- **(A) CD4+ T cells recognizing host MHC class II antigens presented by donor APCs.** Incorrect. Donor APCs are typically depleted or inactivated during conditioning regimens to prevent graft-versus-leukemia (GVL) effects and minimize the risk of donor T cells being activated *against* the donor graft. The primary source of antigen presentation *to* donor T cells in GVHD is the host's own APCs.
- **(B) CD8+ T cells recognizing host MHC class I antigens presented by donor APCs.** Incorrect. Similar to option A, donor APCs are not the primary presenters of host antigens in GVHD.
- **(C) CD4+ T cells recognizing host MHC class II antigens presented by host APCs.** Correct. This describes a key interaction in GVHD. Donor CD4+ T cells recognize host MHC class II molecules presenting peptides (which can be self-peptides or peptides from minor histocompatibility antigens) on the surface of host APCs. This recognition leads to T cell activation, proliferation, and differentiation, initiating the inflammatory cascade.
- **(D) CD8+ T cells recognizing host MHC class I antigens presented by host APCs.** Incorrect. While CD8+ T cells are involved in GVHD and recognize host MHC class I, host APCs are not the primary cells presenting MHC class I antigens to CD8+ T cells in this context. Host cells (e.g., epithelial cells, endothelial cells) present MHC class I antigens to CD8+ T cells, leading to direct cytotoxicity. However, the *initial activation* of donor CD8+ T cells often occurs via cross-presentation by host APCs, but the recognition of host MHC class I by donor CD8+ T cells is a critical effector mechanism, not necessarily the *most critical initiating* interaction compared to the broader activation driven by CD4+ T cell recognition of host MHC II on host APCs. Option C represents the initial T cell activation event more broadly.
- **(E) CD4+ T cells recognizing donor MHC class II antigens presented by host APCs.** Incorrect. The donor T cells are attacking the *host* tissues. They recognize *host* antigens (including host MHC molecules) presented by host APCs, not donor antigens.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- **(A):** This option confuses the roles of donor and host APCs. Donor APCs are generally not the primary activators of donor T cells in GVHD. The conditioning regimen aims to eliminate or inactivate them. The key interaction involves donor T cells recognizing host antigens.
- **(B):** Similar to (A), this option incorrectly identifies donor APCs as the primary antigen presenters to donor T cells.
- **(D):** While CD8+ T cells recognizing host MHC class I is crucial for the cytotoxic effector phase of GVHD, the initial activation and orchestration of the immune response often involve CD4+ T cells recognizing host MHC class II on host APCs. This interaction is considered a fundamental initiating step. Furthermore, host APCs are not the *only* cells presenting MHC class I to CD8+ T cells; host target cells also present MHC class I. Option C describes a more general and critical initiating event involving both CD4+ and CD8+ T cell activation.
- **(E):** This option describes a scenario where donor T cells would recognize the donor graft as foreign, which is not the mechanism of GVHD. GVHD is characterized by donor T cells attacking the host.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature | Graft-versus-Host Disease (GVHD) | Graft-versus-Leukemia (GVL) | Host-versus-Graft Disease (Rejection) |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Immune Cells** | Donor T cells attack Host tissues | Donor T cells attack residual Host leukemia cells | Host T cells attack Donor graft tissues |
| **Antigen Recognition** | Donor T cells recognize Host MHC/minor Ags presented by Host APCs | Donor T cells recognize Leukemia-specific Ags presented by Host APCs | Host T cells recognize Donor MHC/minor Ags presented by Donor APCs (or host APCs presenting donor antigens) |
| **Primary Target** | Skin, Gut, Liver | Leukemia cells | Graft tissues (e.g., skin, bone marrow) |
| **Clinical Manifestations** | Rash, diarrhea, jaundice, liver failure | Remission of leukemia | Graft failure, skin rash, organ dysfunction |
| **Key Cell Interaction** | Donor T cells (CD4+/CD8+) recognize Host APCs presenting Host Ags | Donor T cells recognize Host APCs presenting Leukemia Ags | Host T cells recognize Donor APCs presenting Donor Ags |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
GVHD is mediated by donor T cells recognizing host antigens presented by host APCs. This is a critical distinction from GVL (donor T cells targeting leukemia) and graft rejection (host T cells targeting the graft). Understanding the roles of CD4+ and CD8+ T cells, MHC class I/II presentation, and the source of APCs is key to differentiating these transplant complications. Remember that host APCs are the primary activators of donor T cells in GVHD.

<a id='question-149'></a>

### Question 149: Hypersensitivity and Autoimmune Disease: Part 3 Focus 49 (Immediate)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological mechanisms underlying Type I hypersensitivity reactions (immediate hypersensitivity), specifically the role of IgE, mast cells, and basophils, and differentiating it from other hypersensitivity types or immune responses. (理解I型超敏反应的免疫机制，特别是IgE、肥大细胞和嗜碱性粒细胞的作用，并将其与其他超敏反应或免疫应答区分开来。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old female presents to the emergency department 15 minutes after receiving her first dose of intravenous vancomycin for suspected methicillin-resistant Staphylococcus aureus (MRSA) pneumonia. She reports sudden onset of itching, hives (urticaria), flushing, and difficulty breathing. Her blood pressure is 85/50 mmHg, heart rate is 120 bpm, and respiratory rate is 28 breaths/min. Physical examination reveals diffuse wheezing and angioedema of the lips and tongue. Laboratory tests show elevated serum tryptase levels. The patient has no prior history of allergies or asthma, but she has a known history of systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE) treated with low-dose prednisone. Which of the following is the most likely immunological mechanism responsible for this patient's reaction?

(A) Deposition of immune complexes in small blood vessels, leading to complement activation and neutrophil recruitment.
(B) Binding of drug-specific IgG antibodies to red blood cells, causing complement-mediated hemolysis.
(C) Cross-linking of IgE antibodies bound to FcεRI receptors on mast cells and basophils by the drug or its metabolites.
(D) Activation of cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) that recognize drug-derived peptides presented on MHC class I molecules.
(E) Formation of drug-protein adducts that are recognized as foreign by the immune system, leading to a delayed-type hypersensitivity reaction.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old female with SLE on prednisone.
- **Trigger:** First dose of intravenous vancomycin.
- **Onset:** Rapid (15 minutes) after drug administration.
- **Symptoms:** Itching, hives (urticaria), flushing, dyspnea, wheezing, angioedema, hypotension, tachycardia, tachypnea. These are classic signs and symptoms of anaphylaxis, a severe Type I hypersensitivity reaction.
- **Laboratory Finding:** Elevated serum tryptase. Tryptase is released from mast cells upon activation, confirming mast cell degranulation.
- **Underlying Condition:** SLE, an autoimmune disease, can sometimes be associated with increased risk of allergic reactions, although the direct link here is less relevant than the acute presentation. Prednisone use might slightly blunt the reaction but doesn't prevent it.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the immunological mechanism. The rapid onset, classic anaphylactic symptoms, and elevated tryptase strongly point towards a Type I hypersensitivity reaction mediated by IgE.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Type I Hypersensitivity (Immediate Hypersensitivity):** This reaction is mediated by IgE antibodies.
    - **Sensitization Phase:** Initial exposure to an allergen (in this case, likely vancomycin or a metabolite) leads to the production of allergen-specific IgE by plasma cells (differentiated from B cells).
    - **Effector Phase:** IgE antibodies bind to high-affinity FcεRI receptors on the surface of mast cells and basophils. Upon subsequent exposure to the same allergen, the allergen cross-links the bound IgE molecules on these cells.
    - **Cell Activation:** Cross-linking of IgE-FcεRI complexes triggers intracellular signaling cascades within mast cells and basophils, leading to degranulation.
    - **Mediator Release:** Degranulation releases preformed mediators (e.g., histamine, tryptase, heparin) and newly synthesized mediators (e.g., leukotrienes, prostaglandins, cytokines).
    - **Clinical Effects:** These mediators cause vasodilation (flushing, hypotension), increased vascular permeability (angioedema, hives), smooth muscle contraction (wheezing, bronchospasm), and itching. The rapid onset (minutes) is characteristic.
- **Vancomycin and Hypersensitivity:** Vancomycin is known to cause infusion-related reactions, including anaphylaxis, often attributed to rapid infusion rates, but true IgE-mediated reactions can occur.
- **Tryptase:** A protease stored in mast cell granules and released during degranulation. Elevated serum levels are a specific marker for mast cell activation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Deposition of immune complexes in small blood vessels, leading to complement activation and neutrophil recruitment. This describes a Type III hypersensitivity reaction (e.g., serum sickness, Arthus reaction). Symptoms are typically delayed (hours to days) and involve vasculitis, arthritis, and glomerulonephritis, not the immediate anaphylactic presentation seen here.
- (B): Binding of drug-specific IgG antibodies to red blood cells, causing complement-mediated hemolysis. This describes a Type II hypersensitivity reaction, specifically drug-induced hemolytic anemia. Symptoms include anemia, jaundice, and hemoglobinuria, not the acute anaphylactic symptoms.
- (D): Activation of cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) that recognize drug-derived peptides presented on MHC class I molecules. This describes a Type IV hypersensitivity reaction (delayed-type hypersensitivity, DTH), mediated by T cells. Symptoms are typically delayed (days) and involve skin rashes, fever, and organ damage (e.g., drug-induced liver injury).
- (E): Formation of drug-protein adducts that are recognized as foreign by the immune system, leading to a delayed-type hypersensitivity reaction. This also describes a Type IV hypersensitivity reaction (DTH). The mechanism involves hapten-carrier complex formation, T cell activation, and cytokine release. The onset is delayed (24-72 hours), and manifestations include contact dermatitis or systemic reactions, not the immediate anaphylaxis.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Type I Hypersensitivity (Correct Answer C) | Type II Hypersensitivity (Distractor B) | Type III Hypersensitivity (Distractor A) | Type IV Hypersensitivity (Distractor D & E) |
|----------------------|-------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------|----------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------|
| **Mediator**         | IgE                                       | IgG, IgM                               | Immune Complexes (IgG, IgM)             | T cells (CD4+, CD8+)                      |
| **Effector Cells**   | Mast cells, Basophils                     | Complement, Phagocytes, NK cells       | Neutrophils, Complement                | Macrophages, CTLs                         |
| **Antigen**          | Soluble allergen                         | Cell surface or ECM antigen            | Soluble antigen                        | Soluble or particulate antigen             |
| **Mechanism**        | IgE cross-linking on FcεRI                | Antibody binding to cell/tissue        | Immune complex deposition               | T cell recognition of antigen/MHC         |
| **Onset**            | Immediate (minutes)                       | Hours to days                          | Hours to days                          | Delayed (24-72 hours)                     |
| **Clinical Example** | Anaphylaxis, Allergic rhinitis, Asthma    | Hemolytic anemia, Goodpasture's        | Serum sickness, Arthus reaction         | Contact dermatitis, Tuberculin skin test   |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The rapid onset (minutes) of systemic symptoms like urticaria, angioedema, wheezing, and hypotension, coupled with elevated tryptase, is pathognomonic for Type I (immediate) hypersensitivity, mediated by IgE cross-linking on mast cells and basophils. Differentiate this from Type II (antibody-mediated cytotoxicity), Type III (immune complex deposition), and Type IV (cell-mediated/delayed) hypersensitivity reactions based on onset, effector cells, mediators, and clinical manifestations. Key differentials for rapid reactions include direct mast cell activation (e.g., opioid infusion) or non-immunologic anaphylaxis, but the IgE mechanism is the classic Type I reaction.

### Question 150: Hypersensitivity and Autoimmune Disease: Part 3 Focus 50 (Hypersensitivity)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Differentiating Type I hypersensitivity (IgE-mediated) from Type II hypersensitivity (antibody-mediated cytotoxicity) based on clinical presentation, mechanism, and target cells/tissues. (区分I型过敏反应（IgE介导）和II型过敏反应（抗体介导的细胞毒性）的临床表现、机制和靶细胞/组织。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old female presents to the emergency department 30 minutes after receiving a transfusion of packed red blood cells (PRBCs). She reports sudden onset of severe itching (pruritus), hives (urticaria), flushing, and difficulty breathing (dyspnea). Her blood pressure is 85/50 mmHg, and her heart rate is 120 bpm. Physical examination reveals diffuse wheezing and stridor. Her past medical history is significant for asthma and seasonal allergies. Laboratory tests show a normal complete blood count (CBC) and comprehensive metabolic panel (CMP). A direct antiglobulin test (DAT, Coombs test) performed on the transfused blood sample is negative. The patient is immediately treated with epinephrine, diphenhydramine, and corticosteroids.

Which of the following immunological mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for this patient's acute reaction to the blood transfusion?

(A) Complement-mediated lysis of transfused red blood cells by pre-formed antibodies.
(B) Mast cell degranulation triggered by IgE antibodies cross-linking with allergens on the transfused red blood cells.
(C) T-cell mediated cytotoxicity against transfused red blood cells expressing foreign MHC class I molecules.
(D) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) involving NK cells targeting transfused red blood cells coated with IgG.
(E) Formation of immune complexes deposited in the renal glomeruli, leading to acute kidney injury.

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old female with a history of asthma and seasonal allergies (atopy), suggesting a predisposition to IgE-mediated hypersensitivity.
*   **Timing:** Reaction occurs acutely (30 minutes) after transfusion initiation. This rapid onset is characteristic of Type I hypersensitivity reactions.
*   **Symptoms:** Pruritus, urticaria, flushing, dyspnea, wheezing, stridor, hypotension, and tachycardia are classic signs of anaphylaxis, a severe systemic Type I hypersensitivity reaction.
*   **Diagnostic Test:** Negative Direct Antiglobulin Test (DAT) on the transfused blood rules out hemolytic transfusion reactions mediated by pre-formed antibodies (like ABO incompatibility) or antibodies developed post-transfusion (like alloimmunization), which are typically Type II or Type III hypersensitivity reactions involving red blood cell destruction.
*   **Treatment:** Response to epinephrine (alpha-agonist for vasoconstriction, beta-agonist for bronchodilation), diphenhydramine (H1 antihistamine), and corticosteroids (anti-inflammatory) is typical for anaphylaxis.

These clues strongly point towards a Type I hypersensitivity reaction (anaphylaxis) triggered by something in the transfused blood product, likely an allergen causing IgE cross-linking on mast cells.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **Type I Hypersensitivity (Immediate Hypersensitivity):** Mediated by IgE antibodies. Upon initial exposure to an allergen, B cells differentiate into plasma cells producing allergen-specific IgE. This IgE binds to high-affinity FcεRI receptors on mast cells and basophils. Subsequent exposure to the same allergen causes cross-linking of the bound IgE molecules, triggering mast cell degranulation. Degranulation releases pre-formed mediators (histamine, heparin, tryptase) and newly synthesized mediators (leukotrienes, prostaglandins, cytokines). These mediators cause vasodilation, increased vascular permeability, smooth muscle contraction (bronchoconstriction), and inflammation, leading to symptoms like urticaria, angioedema, bronchospasm, hypotension, and anaphylactic shock.
*   **Type II Hypersensitivity (Cytotoxic Hypersensitivity):** Mediated by IgG or IgM antibodies directed against antigens on cell surfaces or extracellular matrix. Mechanisms include complement-mediated lysis (e.g., ABO incompatibility transfusion reaction), antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) by NK cells or macrophages, or antibody-mediated cellular dysfunction (e.g., Graves' disease, Myasthenia Gravis). A positive DAT is characteristic of many Type II reactions involving red blood cells.
*   **Type III Hypersensitivity (Immune Complex Hypersensitivity):** Mediated by the deposition of antigen-antibody (immune) complexes in tissues, leading to complement activation and inflammation (e.g., serum sickness, Arthus reaction, post-streptococcal glomerulonephritis).
*   **Type IV Hypersensitivity (Delayed-Type Hypersensitivity):** Mediated by T cells (specifically Th1 cells and cytotoxic T lymphocytes). It involves cytokine release (e.g., IFN-γ) leading to macrophage activation and inflammation, or direct cytotoxicity by CTLs (e.g., contact dermatitis, tuberculin skin test, graft rejection).

In this case, the rapid onset, symptoms of anaphylaxis (urticaria, wheezing, hypotension), history of atopy, and negative DAT are hallmarks of a Type I hypersensitivity reaction. While transfusion reactions can be Type II (hemolytic), Type III (serum sickness), or Type IV (graft-versus-host disease), the clinical picture here is classic for Type I. The allergen could be a component of the blood product, such as plasma proteins or preservatives, to which the patient has pre-existing IgE.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Complement-mediated lysis of transfused red blood cells by pre-formed antibodies.** This describes a Type II hypersensitivity reaction, specifically an acute hemolytic transfusion reaction (e.g., ABO incompatibility). This would typically present with fever, chills, flank pain, hemoglobinuria, and a positive DAT. The negative DAT and the specific symptoms (urticaria, wheezing) make this unlikely.
- (C): **T-cell mediated cytotoxicity against transfused red blood cells expressing foreign MHC class I molecules.** This describes a Type IV hypersensitivity reaction, specifically graft-versus-host disease (GVHD) occurring in an immunocompromised host receiving blood products containing viable lymphocytes. GVHD typically presents later (days to weeks) with fever, rash, diarrhea, and liver dysfunction. It is not consistent with the acute anaphylactic presentation.
- (D): **Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) involving NK cells targeting transfused red blood cells coated with IgG.** This is another mechanism of Type II hypersensitivity. It involves IgG antibodies coating target cells, which are then recognized and killed by NK cells via FcγRIII (CD16). This is typically seen in hemolytic anemia or transfusion reactions involving alloantibodies, often associated with a positive DAT (though not always). It does not explain the immediate anaphylactic symptoms.
- (E): **Formation of immune complexes deposited in the renal glomeruli, leading to acute kidney injury.** This describes a Type III hypersensitivity reaction. While transfusion reactions can sometimes cause serum sickness (a Type III reaction), it typically presents days to weeks after the transfusion with fever, rash, arthralgias, and potentially glomerulonephritis. The acute onset and specific symptoms (urticaria, wheezing, hypotension) are not characteristic of Type III hypersensitivity.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Type I Hypersensitivity (Anaphylaxis) | Type II Hypersensitivity (Hemolysis) | Type III Hypersensitivity (Immune Complex) | Type IV Hypersensitivity (GVHD) |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------ | :----------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------- | :----------------------------- |
| **Mediator**            | IgE                                   | IgG, IgM                             | Immune Complexes (IgG, IgM)               | T cells                        |
| **Mechanism**           | Mast cell degranulation               | Complement lysis, ADCC, Dysfunction  | Complement activation, Inflammation       | Cytokine release, Cytotoxicity |
| **Onset**               | Minutes                                | Minutes to hours                     | Days to weeks                             | Days to weeks                 |
| **Key Symptoms**        | Urticaria, wheezing, hypotension       | Fever, chills, hemoglobinuria        | Fever, rash, arthralgias, glomerulonephritis | Fever, rash, diarrhea          |
| **DAT Result**          | Negative                               | Often Positive                       | Negative                                  | Negative                       |
| **Example (Transfusion)** | Allergic reaction to plasma protein   | ABO incompatibility                  | Serum sickness                             | Transfusion-associated GVHD    |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The key takeaway is recognizing the clinical presentation of anaphylaxis (rapid onset, urticaria, wheezing, hypotension) as characteristic of Type I hypersensitivity. Differentiating Type I from other hypersensitivity types (especially Type II hemolytic reactions) relies on understanding the underlying mechanism (IgE vs. IgG/IgM), the typical onset time, and the presence or absence of specific diagnostic findings like a positive DAT. Remember that atopy (asthma, allergies) increases the risk of Type I reactions.
